=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer array nums and an integer k.
An integer h is called valid if all values in the array that are strictly greater than h are identical.
For example, if nums = [10, 8, 10, 8], a valid integer is h = 9 because all nums[i] > 9 are equal to 10, but 5 is not a valid integer.
You are allowed to perform the following operation on nums:

Select an integer h that is valid for the current values in nums.
For each index i where nums[i] > h, set nums[i] to h.

Return the minimum number of operations required to make every element in nums equal to k. If it is impossible to make all elements equal to k, return -1.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [5,2,5,4,5], k = 2
Output: 2
Explanation:
The operations can be performed in order using valid integers 4 and then 2.

Example 2:

Input: nums = [2,1,2], k = 2
Output: -1
Explanation:
It is impossible to make all the values equal to 2.

Example 3:

Input: nums = [9,7,5,3], k = 1
Output: 4
Explanation:
The operations can be performed using valid integers in the order 7, 5, 3, and 1.

 
Constraints:

1 <= nums.length <= 100 
1 <= nums[i] <= 100
1 <= k <= 100

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        if min(nums) < k:
            return -1
        return len(set(x for x in nums if x > k))

Now, final code:

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        if min(nums) < k:
            return -1
        return len(set(x for x in nums if x > k))
```

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        if min(nums) < k:

Let's think if there is any hidden issue with "all values in the array that are strictly greater than h are identical" if h is valid but h is not necessarily less than max. If h >= max, no values >h, valid. Operation no effect. We don't count. Good.

Now, maybe if h is valid but h is less than max and values >h identical, operation sets max to h. If h is not equal to second max but greater than second max, max becomes a new value between old max and second max. This can be useful? It doesn't reduce D, but maybe could reduce number of operations by allowing a later operation to lower multiple values? Let's explore to ensure no counterexample where creating new values reduces operations below D? Lower bound says no because each operation reduces D by at most 1. But wait, if h is a new value >k, D remains same, not decrease. If h is a new value >k, it could later be lowered together with another value? Could two distinct values ever be lowered in one operation? No, because valid h requires all values >h identical, so only one distinct value above h. Thus an operation can never lower two distinct values simultaneously. So each distinct value that ever exists above k must be eliminated by a separate operation. Creating new distinct values above k only adds more values to eliminate, not fewer. So no.

But let's examine lower bound more carefully: Initial D distinct values >k. Each operation can reduce D by at most 1, but could it reduce D by 1 while also "merging" with a value that is not currently >k? If h=k, it reduces by 1. If h<k, impossible. If h>k new, no reduction. If h>k existing, reduction by 1. So to go from D to 0, need at least D operations. This is solid. It doesn't matter if new values created; they only increase required operations. So optimal is D.

Let's maybe think if there is any scenario where an operation reduces D by 1 but also changes a value <k to >k? No, operation never increases. So no.

Now, maybe if h is valid and h is less than k, D might reduce by more than 1? Example values {5,4,3}, k=2, choose h=1? invalid because values >1 are 5,4,3 not identical. But impossible final. So successful sequence won't. Lower bound for successful sequence with h>=k. Good.

Now, maybe if h<k and there is only one distinct value >h, operation can lower all to h<k, D becomes0, but final impossible. So not relevant.

Now, maybe if h<k and there are values >h identical, operation could lower all values to h<k, then maybe choose h=k? no effect. impossible. Good.

Now, maybe if h<k and there are values >h identical, operation could lower all values to h<k, then choose h' between h and k? If all values equal h<k, choose h' > h: no values >h', no effect. choose h' < h: lower. no raise. impossible. Good.

Now, maybe if h<k and there are values >h identical, operation could lower all values to h<k, but if h is not less than all? no.

Let's think if there is any hidden issue with "valid integer
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        if min(nums) < k:
            return -1
        return len(set(x for x in nums if x > k))
```
